=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple connected undirected graph with N vertices and M edges, where the vertices are numbered 1 to N and the edges are numbered 1 to M. Edge i connects vertex u_i and vertex v_i in both directions.
Initially, there is a piece A on vertex S and a piece B on vertex T. Here, S and T are given as input.
You may perform the following operation any number of times in any order:

- Choose either piece A or piece B, and move it from its current vertex to an adjacent vertex via an edge. However, you cannot make a move that results in both pieces ending up on the same vertex.

Your goal is to reach the state in which piece A is on vertex T and piece B is on vertex S.
Determine whether this is possible, and if it is, find the minimum number of operations required to achieve it.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M S T
u_1 v_1
u_2 v_2
\vdots
u_M v_M

Output

If it is impossible to achieve the goal, print -1.
If it is possible, print the minimum number of operations required.

Constraints


- 2 \le N \le 2\times 10^5
- \displaystyle N-1 \le M \le \min\left(\frac{N(N-1)}{2},\,2\times 10^5\right)
- 1 \le u_i < v_i \le N
- The given graph is simple and connected.
- 1 \le S, T \le N
- S \neq T
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 4 3 4
2 4
1 4
3 4
2 3

Sample Output 1

3

For example, the following sequence of operations completes the goal in three moves:

- Move piece A to vertex 2.
- Piece A is on vertex 2, piece B is on vertex 4.


- Move piece B to vertex 3.
- Piece A is on vertex 2, piece B is on vertex 3.


- Move piece A to vertex 4.
- Piece A is on vertex 4, piece B is on vertex 3.



It is impossible to complete the goal in fewer than three moves, so print 3.

Sample Input 2

2 1 1 2
1 2

Sample Output 2

-1

No matter how you move the pieces, you cannot achieve the goal.

Sample Input 3

5 6 3 5
1 2
2 3
1 5
2 4
1 3
2 5

Sample Output 3

4

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's consider the "block-cut tree" (also called bridge tree? Actually biconnected components). In a graph, cycles are within biconnected components (blocks) that are not bridges. Let's step back and seek a general algorithm.

Given constraints 2e5, maybe we can compute shortest path in configuration graph using BFS on a graph of size O(N) by considering "important" states? Perhaps the distance can be found by running Dijkstra on the original graph with state = position of A and B? No.

Maybe there is a known result: The distance between (S,T) and (T,S) in the two-token graph equals the length of the shortest path between S and T in the graph after adding an edge? Or equals 2 * dist_{G'}(S,T) where G' is the "bipartite double cover" of G? Let's explore.

Consider the "symmetric product" and "configuration space". There is a map from the configuration space of ordered pairs to the unordered pair plus a bit (the order). This suggests the key is the block-cut tree and the ability to move tokens into different branches of an articulation.

Let's model the graph as a tree of biconnected components. At an articulation vertex, there are multiple "branches" (blocks/components). A token can move from one branch to another through the articulation. If two tokens need to pass through an articulation in opposite directions, they can avoid collision if there is at least one branch (other than the two they use) where a token can wait? In triangle+leaf, articulation1 has branches: leaf4 and cycle block (which provides two incident edges 1-2,1-3). To swap A=4 and B=1, B moved into branch 1-2, A moved to1, then A moved into branch1-3, B moved to1, B to4, A to1. The articulation has degree in the block-cut tree: leaf branch and cyclic block with two "directions" (2 and3) inside the same block. The cycle block allowed two distinct exits from articulation within the same block, letting them pass.

In a simple path, articulation vertices have degree 2 in the block-cut tree (two incident edges), no extra branch, so impossible. In a tree, every articulation has only the two path directions, no alternative, so impossible. In any graph with a cycle, some articulation/block has an alternative, enabling passage.

Maybe the problem can be reduced to the "block-cut tree" where each block is either a bridge (an edge) or a cyclic block. Within a cyclic block, tokens can move between any two vertices? Actually a biconnected component with at least one cycle allows two distinct paths between any two vertices in the block (by Menger), so tokens can pass within the block. The cost to pass through a cyclic block may be related to distances within the block.

Let's consider the "block-cut tree" BCT:
- Nodes are blocks (biconnected components) and articulation vertices.
- Edges connect a block node to an articulation vertex contained in it.
- Original vertices that are not articulation belong to exactly one block.
- A bridge is a block consisting of two vertices and one edge; it's a tree edge in BCT.
- A cyclic block is a biconnected component with at least 3 vertices/cycle.

Tokens move between blocks through articulation vertices. Since within a cyclic block, there is freedom to choose paths and pass; within a bridge block, tokens just move along the edge.

Maybe the shortest swap can be computed on the BCT with weights:
- Moving a token along a bridge edge costs 1.
- Passing through a cyclic block from one articulation (or vertex) to another costs the shortest path distance within the block between the entry/exit vertices.
- Additionally, a cyclic block can serve as a "waiting/passing area" with some cost.

But the state still has two tokens on the BCT. However, the BCT is a tree (if we ignore cyclic block internal structure). On a tree, two tokens cannot pass. Cyclic blocks are like "nodes" with the ability to let tokens pass each other. The cost to swap two tokens in a tree with some "passing nodes" might be computable by tree DP.

Maybe each cyclic block can be treated as a "switch" that allows the order of tokens along the BCT to be reversed at a cost equal to the length of the shortest cycle within that block? Let's test.

But tail example S=4,T=5: the path in BCT from S to T is just bridge block 4-5, no cyclic block on the path. Yet swap possible using a cyclic block off the path (attached at articulation1). So tokens can leave the path to use an off-path cyclic block. Thus we need consider distances from the path to cyclic blocks.

This is analogous to: On a tree (the BCT), tokens cannot pass. To swap, one token must go from the path to a cyclic block (a "passing place") and back, changing the order. Let's consider using BFS on the original graph to compute distances from S and T to all vertices, and maybe answer is min over edges/vertices of something. Let's try to derive a formula from the "two paths" perspective.

Any valid sequence gives two walks: A from S to T of length p, B from T to S of length q, total L=p+q. The moves can be scheduled. What are the necessary and sufficient conditions on the two walks for schedulability?

Suppose we have two walks P (A) and Q (B). We can interleave their edges. Since moves are sequential, the only constraint is that at every time, the current positions differ. If we choose an interleaving, the positions are prefixes of P and Q. This is equivalent to finding a path in the grid from (0,0) to (p,q) where at each step increment one coordinate, and the corresponding vertices P[i], Q[j] are distinct. We can choose P,Q and the grid path. This is like two strings over vertices; need avoid equal pairs. We want minimize p+q.

This resembles finding the shortest path in the "product" of two walks. If P and Q are simple paths, can we always schedule unless they share vertices in a conflicting way? Let's analyze.

Given two walks P (S->T) and Q (T->S). If we take the natural alternating schedule? We can always choose to move A along P while B waits, but if B's current vertex blocks A's next step, need move B first. This is like the "token swapping on a path" problem.

Maybe the minimal total moves equals the minimum over two walks P,Q of p+q such that there exists a "noncrossing" condition. This is equivalent to the length of the shortest path in the "free product" of the two walks. This is essentially a two-token problem on a tree with branch vertices. Maybe we can solve it by reducing to a path with "passing places" at branch vertices. Each branch vertex of degree>=3 can serve as a passing place. The tokens move along the unique path between S and T; side branches allow one token to step off the path. The cost to pass at a branch vertex v might be:
- If both tokens are on opposite sides of v, they can pass with extra cost 2 * min(dist from v to nearest token?)?
Let's model the path P between S and T as a line. There are some "passing vertices" on P where a token can step off into a side branch. Initially tokens at endpoints S,T. They need exchange positions along the line. This is like two tokens on a path with "sidings" at certain vertices. A siding at v allows a token to move off the path (one step) and back, letting the other pass. What is the minimum number of moves?

This is a classic problem: two trains on a single track with sidings. The minimum time to swap trains is 2D + 2 * (distance from the siding to the nearest endpoint?)? Let's derive.

Suppose path positions 0..D, tokens at 0 and D. There are sidings at positions p_i (vertices with extra branch). A token at a siding can move into the siding (1 move) and later return, while the other passes through the siding. To swap, at least one token must use a siding. If a siding is at position p, the token that uses it must travel from its current side to p, enter siding, let other pass, exit, continue. The total extra cost might be 2 * min(p, D-p)? Let's test.

- Star leaf-leaf: path positions S=0, T=2 (via center p=1). Siding at p=1. min(p, D-p)=1. Extra over 2D=2? 2*min=2, answer6 = 2D+2. Good.
- Star center-leaf: path positions S=0? Let S=leaf at0, T=center at1. Siding at T=1 (endpoint). min(p=1, D-p=0)=0, but answer6 = 2D+4. Not.
Maybe if siding at endpoint, extra 4; if internal, extra 2*min? For p-q-T example S=p=0, T=2, sidings at p=0 and q=1. Choose siding at p=0 endpoint? min=0, but answer5 = 2D+1. Hmm.

Let's compute using "siding at position p" cost formula. Suppose token A starts at 0, B at D. To swap, one token (say A) uses siding at p. So the general problem is complex but maybe can be solved by computing the shortest path in the "block tree" with edge weights and using a DP for two tokens on a tree with "special nodes" that have costs.

Let's consider the block-cut tree (BCT) of the graph. Each block is either:
- A bridge edge (a path of length1 between two articulation vertices or leaf).
- A cyclic block (biconnected component with at least one cycle).

Within a cyclic block, tokens can move freely; the cost to traverse between two vertices in the block is the shortest path distance in the block. More importantly, a cyclic block can serve as a "passing area" with a certain cost depending on entry/exit vertices.

Maybe we can compress each cyclic block into a "supernode" with internal distances. The BCT is a tree. The two tokens move on this tree, but when they enter a cyclic block, they can "pass" each other at some cost. This is analogous to a tree with some nodes being "passing nodes" that allow tokens to reverse order. The cost to pass at a passing node might be the length of the shortest cycle in the block that contains the two incident articulation vertices? Let's test.

For a cyclic block that is a simple cycle of length L, if the path between S and T enters the block at vertex a and exits at b (a,b on the block), the cost to pass through the block (i.e., swap the order of tokens within the block) might be the length of the shortest cycle in the block containing a,b, which is L (the whole cycle). But if S,T are both in the block, answer = L. If the block is a triangle and S,T are in different branches attached to a vertex r of the block, the entry/exit for the path S-T is r on both sides? This suggests a general algorithm based on the block-cut tree where each cyclic block has a "local swap cost" that depends on the two entry branches:
- If the S-T path in the BCT enters the cyclic block through two different articulation vertices (or one vertex and the other outside?), the cost to pass through the block is the length of the shortest cycle in the block connecting those two entry/exit vertices? - If tokens approach from different branches: local cost = 2.
- If one at r: local cost = 4 (star center-leaf).
- If same branch: impossible (because no cycle to provide two side branches? Wait if same branch, the branch vertex is off the path; tree branch off path impossible. So local cost infinite).
For a cyclic block at r:
- Different branches: local cost = L (or shortest cycle length through r?).
- One at r: local cost = 4.
- Same branch: local cost = 4 (possible because the block provides two side branches even though the path doesn't go through r; tokens must travel to r and use it).

This is promising! The graph can be decomposed into a tree of "nodes" (articulation vertices and blocks). The cost to swap two tokens can be computed by considering the path between S and T in this tree and the nearest "passing node" (a branch vertex or cyclic block) that can be used.

Let's formalize the BCT approach.

Construct block-cut tree:
- Original vertices that are articulation points become nodes.
- Each biconnected component (block) becomes a node.
- Edges connect an articulation node to a block node if the articulation belongs to the block.
- Non-articulation vertices belong to exactly one block; S or T may be non-articulation. We can treat them as attached to their block with zero distance? Or include all original vertices as nodes? Let's try to fully solve the tree case first, then extend to graphs with cycles by treating each cycle as a "super vertex" with local costs. Maybe the problem can be reduced to a tree (the block-cut tree) where each node has a "type":
- Articulation vertex with degree d in BCT.
- Cyclic block node with girth g and distances to incident articulations.

The two tokens move on this tree. The cost to swap can be computed by considering the path between S and T in the BCT and the nearest "passing node" (a cyclic block or a branch articulation). This is analogous to the tree formula but with passing nodes having a "cost" and "distance" from the path.

For a tree (no cyclic blocks), passing nodes are articulation vertices with degree>=3. The formula we derived:
Let P be the S-T path in the tree. If no vertex on P has degree>=3, -1.
Let v be a vertex on P with degree>=3. If v is internal (not S/T), answer = 2D + 2. If v is an endpoint, answer = 2D + 4. If there are multiple, choose internal if possible, else endpoint. This can be unified as:
answer = 2D + min_{v on P, deg>=3} (2 if v not in {S,T} else 4).
What if both S and T are branch vertices and there is no internal branch? Then choose either endpoint, answer 2D+4. Example S=center,T=center? impossible. If S and T both branch vertices adjacent? D=1, answer? Tree: center S connected to center T? Two branch vertices connected by edge, each with extra leaves. S=center1,T=center2. D=1. Can swap? Try sequence: A center1, B center2. A can move to leaf, B center2->center1, A leaf->center2? Let's simulate: A1->a, B2->1, A a->? a only1 occupied, stuck. Need both use extra leaves. Likely answer6 =2D+4. Formula endpoint branch gives6. Good.

Now for a graph with cycles, the block-cut tree has cyclic block nodes. A cyclic block node can serve as a passing node. The "distance" from the S-T path to a cyclic block node in the BCT and the local cost depends on whether the block is on the path or off.

Maybe we can transform each cyclic block into a "branch vertex" with a local cost equal to its girth if the path goes through it, or 4 if off-path. But tail cycle off-path local cost4, leaf-leaf attached to r off-path? Actually the cyclic block is off the S-T path? In leaf-leaf, path S-r-T; the cyclic block is attached to r but not on the path. This depends on the path's relation to the attachment vertex.

This is essentially the same as the tree formula if we treat the cyclic block as a "branch" at r with cost girth for different branches and 4 for same/endpoint. For a tree branch vertex, different branches cost2, endpoint cost4, same impossible. For a cyclic block, different branches cost girth, endpoint/same cost4. So a cyclic block at r provides an additional "branch" with cost girth when the tokens are on different sides of r, and cost4 when not.

Thus the graph can be seen as a tree (the block-cut tree) where each articulation vertex r may have some "passing branches" (tree branches with cost2, cyclic blocks with cost girth). The tokens need to swap along the path; they can use any passing branch attached to a vertex on the path (or reachable by detour). The cost formula might be:
answer = 2D + min over passing branches b attached to a vertex v on or off path of [ 2*dist(path, v) + cost_branch ],

- For a tree branch at v (degree>=3): if v is on the path and the branch is not along the path, cost = 2 if v is internal and tokens are on different sides? Hmm.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
from collections import deque

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, M, S, T = map(int, input().split())
    S -= 1
    T -= 1
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    edges = []
    for _ in range(M):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1
        v -= 1
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))

    # BFS distances from S and T
    def bfs(src):
        dist = [-1] * N
        dist[src] = 0
        q = deque([src])
        while q:
            v = q.popleft()
            for to in adj[v]:
                if dist[to] == -1:
                    dist[to] = dist[v] + 1
                    q.append(to)
        return dist

    distS = bfs(S)
    distT = bfs(T)
    D = distS[T]

    # ---------- tree case ----------
    if M == N - 1:
        # parent BFS from S to T
        parent = [-1] * N
        dist = [-1] * N
        dist[S] = 0
        q = deque([S])
        while q:
            v = q.popleft()
            if v == T:
                break
            for to in adj[v]:
                if dist[to] == -1:
                    dist[to] = dist[v] + 1
                    parent[to] = v
                    q.append(to)

        path = []
        cur = T
        while cur != -1:
            path.append(cur)
            cur = parent[cur]
        path.reverse()

        branch = [v for v in path if len(adj[v]) >= 3]
        if not branch:
            print(-1)
            return

        internal = [v for v in branch if v != S and v != T]
        if internal:
            print(2 * D + 2)
        else:
            print(2 * D + 4)
        return

    # ---------- non-tree case ----------
    # Build BFS tree from S and LCA to find the shortest cycle containing S and T.
    LOG = (N).bit_length()
    up = [[-1] * N for _ in range(LOG)]
    depth = [-1] * N
    depth[S] = 0
    q = deque([S])
    parent = [-1] * N
    order = [S]
    while q:
        v = q.popleft()
        for to in adj[v]:
            if depth[to] == -1:
                depth[to] = depth[v] + 1
                parent[to] = v
                up[0][to] = v
                q.append(to)
                order.append(to)

    for k in range(1, LOG):
        prev = up[k - 1]
        cur = up[k]
        for v in range(N):
            if prev[v] != -1:
                cur[v] = prev[prev[v]]

    def lca(a, b):
        if depth[a] < depth[b]:
            a, b = b, a
        diff = depth[a] - depth[b]
        bit = 0
        while diff:
            if diff & 1:
                a = up[bit][a]
            diff >>= 1
            bit += 1
        if a == b:
            return a
        for k in range(LOG - 1, -1, -1):
            if up[k][a] != up[k][b]:
                a = up[k][a]
                b = up[k][b]
        return up[0][a]

    def tree_dist(a, b):
        c = lca(a, b)
        return depth[a] + depth[b] - 2 * depth[c]

    INF = 10**18
    best_cycle = INF

    tree_edge = set()
    for v in range(N):
        if parent[v] != -1:
            tree_edge.add((v, parent[v]))
            tree_edge.add((parent[v], v))

    for u, v in edges:
        if (u, v) in tree_edge:
            continue
        # T lies on the tree path between u and v?
        if tree_dist(u, T) + tree_dist(T, v) == tree_dist(u, v):
            cand = distS[u] + distS[v] + 1
            if cand < best_cycle:
                best_cycle = cand

    if best_cycle < INF:
        print(best_cycle)
    else:
        # No simple cycle contains both S and T.
        # In this case a small extra detour is necessary.
        print(2 * D + 1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
